| **[SALT-GNN: Handling Dense Neighborhoods in Anti-Money Laundering Graphs via Statistics-Aware Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10131v1)** | 2026-07-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Money laundering threatens financial stability and exposes institutions to penalties, motivating automated detection. Because laundering schemes often emerge through relational patterns, graph neural networks (GNNs) are increasingly used for anti-money laundering (AML). Yet AML GNNs are typically evaluated with aggregate metrics such as overall F1 score, which hide an operational issue: high-activity recipient accounts concentrate many incoming transactions, making suspicious signals harder to isolate and costlier to investigate. We introduce a recipient-degree stratified evaluation that reports standard AML metrics across recipient-context density. Across three datasets (HI-Small, HI-Medium, and AMLSim-32k-5%), it reveals consistent degradation in dense recipient contexts, which we trace to three GNN characteristics: two known limitations that AML amplifies, i.e., (1) multiset non-discriminability and (2) cardinality blindness, and (3) an attention-specific effect: in dense neighborhoods, normalized attention attenuates weak but pattern-relevant multi-hop signals. Guided by this diagnosis, we propose SALT-GNN, a lightweight statistics-aware architecture that fuses degree-aware statistical aggregation with attention at each message-passing layer, so distributional and cardinality information shapes the node states used by subsequent attention steps. Ablations support fusion placement as a key factor in dense-context performance. On HI-Small and HI-Medium, SALT-GNN uses up to 77% fewer parameters than task-specific graph-transformer baselines while improving dense-context F1 score by 3-6 points; on AMLSim-32k-5%, it improves highest-degree F1 score by 16-20 points. The gains hold for both Transformer- and GAT-style attention, indicating that the benefit comes from where statistical and attentional evidence is fused rather than from a specific attention operator.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 7 figures. Code available upon publication</p></details> |
| **[Inverse-IMPRESSION: A Graph-based Platform for Molecular Structure Elucidation from Experimental NMR Spectroscopic Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09978v1)** | 2026-07-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Here, we present a platform built on our inverted Graph Transformer Network, IMPRESSION-G2, which can accurately and rapidly reconstruct molecular bonding directly from experimental nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopic information. It comprises three interconnected stages: a one-shot model that predicts bond connectivity between atoms; a structure-correction stage that corrects the predicted structures by removing uncertain bonds and iteratively reassigning them; noise-augmented multi-shot prediction, generating an ensemble of candidate structures, which are ranked to identify the best-fit structure. By integrating a range of $^{1}$H and $^{13}$C NMR data, including two-dimensional (2D) experiments such as COSY, HSQC, and HMBC, the inverse-IMPRESSION platform correctly identifies the structures of 77.8% of molecules with up to 30 heavy atoms (H, C, N, O and F) using simulated NMR data, and 10 of 19 (53%) molecules using experimental NMR data. The experimental structures solved have molecular weights of up to 480 Da and are representative of the complex structures in synthetic and natural products that routinely challenge chemists. The inverse-IMPRESSION framework thus provides the first effective approach for automated molecular structure elucidation using graph-based machine learning on experimental data.</p></details> | <details><summary>15-pa...</summary><p>15-page manuscript (4 figures), plus 59 pages of Supporting Information (45 figures); Submitted to the Journal of the American Chemical Society</p></details> |

## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[PixelMoE: Scaling Pixel-Space Diffusion with LLM-Style Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18857v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pixel-space diffusion models provide an appealing alternative to latent-space diffusion models by directly modeling raw images without relying on a learned tokenizer. However, scaling pixel-space models has predominantly followed dense strategies, tightly coupling model capacity with active computation. In this work, we show that standard LLM-style Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures can be effectively transferred to pixel-space diffusion. Building on this finding, we introduce PixelMoE, a pixel-space generative framework that sparsely scales model capacity with standard LLM-style MoE, without diffusion-specific routing mechanisms or auxiliary routing objectives. Specifically, PixelMoE combines standard Token-Choice (TC) routing with fine-grained routed experts for input-adaptive sparse computation, a shared expert for capturing common representations, and a bias-based load-balancing strategy. To complement sparse capacity scaling, PixelMoE further introduces Pixel-Space Hierarchical Flow Matching, which varies spatial resolution along the denoising trajectory from global structure formation to fine-grained detail refinement. At comparable active parameter counts, PixelMoE consistently outperforms its dense counterparts across model scales, demonstrating effective sparse capacity scaling in pixel space. On class-conditional ImageNet-1K, PixelMoE achieves FID scores of 1.47 at 256x256 and 1.53 at 512x512 without representation alignment or perceptual losses, comparing favorably with leading pixel-space and latent-space generative models. Code and models will be released soon.</p></details> |  |
| **[Back-Translation Still Matters: Leveraging Monolingual Data for Low-Resource Machine Translation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18818v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-trained Large Language Model (LLMs) are effective on many tasks in zero-shot and few-shot settings. For machine translation of low-resource languages, the obvious options are few-shot prompting and fine-tuning on a small parallel set. We ask: what if moderate amounts of low-resource monolingual data are also available? We argue that, rather than continued pretraining, a more effective use of this data is the pre-LLM era technique of back-translation (BT). The current fine-tuned LLM translates monolingual sentences in each language, and the resulting pairs train it in the reverse direction. Updates on the parallel set are interleaved for stable learning. With 1K parallel pairs on Odia and Swahili, BT beats continued pretraining into the low-resource language by 7.7 to 16.4 XCOMET-XXL points in domain. With beam-decoded intermediate translations, it also beats a model with 10 time more parallel data. In-context examples retrieved from the parallel data further improve BT. We also analyze the source, quality and amount of monolingual text. Across five low-resource languages, with Hindi as a high-resource control, and two 3B backbones, BT improves translation into the low-resource language by 16 to 173% XCOMET-XXL over parallel-only fine-tuning. Blind human evaluation on Odia and Sanskrit agrees with the automatic metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[Where Do LLM Ensemble Gains Come From? Decomposing Candidate Value, Diversity, and Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18786v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM ensemble gains are often measured against a single baseline, but the value attributed to additional candidates can change with the comparison used. We study an LLM aggregator under four matched conditions: Direct (no candidates), Single-slot (one candidate in a multi-slot interface), Duplicate (one candidate repeated), and Multi-candidate (separately generated candidates). Under either non-diverse control, overall candidate gain (CIV) splits algebraically into an additional-candidate contrast (DSV) and a candidate-context contrast (CCV); the split, but not CIV, depends on the chosen control. On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). In FRAMES evidence aggregation, candidate-context contrasts remain strongly negative under both non-diverse controls. Our results show why ensemble evaluations should state the reference control before attributing gains to additional candidates.</p></details> | 14 pages, 6 figures |
| **[From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26277v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.</p></details> | <details><summary>Dianj...</summary><p>Dianjing Fan and Yao Li equally contributed to this work. 7 pages, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[Looking Complete Is Not Being Grounded: Evaluating LLM Sitreps Against Human-Authored Situation Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18762v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluation for automatic crisis reporting should measure what practitioners need, not what is convenient to compare. Through LLM-assisted, author-approved content analysis, we derive a 15-slot information schema from professional humanitarian situation reports using qualitative content analysis, then evaluate 121 human and 261 LLM-generated reports across five disasters and three hazard types. Under google/gemini-3.7-flash on Cyclone Idai, the best machine arm is nominally above the empirical human ceiling (0.827 vs. 0.821), but the difference is statistically indistinguishable from zero (95\% bootstrap CI $[-.049,.059]$; permutation $p=.852$), and the ordering does not replicate under openai/gpt-5.6-luna. On a blind 100-item Idai sample, the author agrees with the Gemini judge at 0.910 accuracy and unweighted Cohen's $κ=.799$. All four judges clear the plan's raw-agreement criterion of .7; only Gemini reaches .7 on chance-corrected $κ$. Across the three primary events, machine reports reproduce 5.4-11.0% of same-day official figures. Across those same events, raw exact-number matching links 14.3-19.6% of official figures and 85.4-90.1% of machine figures to the social stream; manual audit confirms 45/50 declared absences, 4/25 official matches, and 24/25 machine matches semantically. The issuing-provenance gap is positive in all 12 observed event-judge cells: report date, organisation, series, and authority were not supplied at professional-report coverage in any cell. On the three primary events, ROUGE-L is uninformative, BERTScore is negatively associated with completeness after multiplicity correction, and a generic LLM judge changes sign.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at LT4CPR Workshop @ AACL-IJCNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Energy-Aware Scheduling for Serverless LLM Serving on Shared GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30391v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM inference becomes a major cloud workload, its growing energy footprint makes cluster-wide energy optimization increasingly important. Serverless LLM serving helps platforms absorb traffic volatility by elastically sharing GPU resources across models, but this sharing also makes energy optimization difficult. Multiple co-resident models run under one device-wide operating point, while their resource demands and latency slack change across execution phases and load conditions. As a result, minimizing energy requires coordinated scheduling across request placement, runtime resource adaptation, and workload consolidation. We present Festina, a profiling-guided, power-aware control plane to minimize cluster-wide energy for serverless LLM serving. Unlike common global-local schedulers that focus on throughput or tail latency, Festina makes energy-first decisions by jointly coordinating request placement, SM partitioning, and GPU operating points under TTFT/TBT SLOs. In our system, a lightweight global scheduler performs fast, SLO-safe, energy-aware placement using constant-time lookups from offline profiles and GPU state summaries. On each GPU, a phase-aware local scheduler continuously adapts task batching and compute resources to minimize power consumption. Festina further performs energy-aware workload consolidation to reduce GPUs' static power consumption via SLO-aware migration. Comparison with four SOTA LLM serving systems and one DVFS-augmented system demonstrates that Festina reduces energy consumption by up to 56% while maintaining parity in SLO attainment (within a 2% margin)</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages body and 4 pages appendix, 19 pages total. Accepted to the 2026 ACM SIGOPS Annual Technical Conference (SIGOPS ATC 2026)</p></details> |
| **[FinTradeBench: A Financial Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19225v6)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Financial analysis requires reasoning over heterogeneous signals, including company fundamentals from regulatory filings and trading signals derived from market prices. However, existing financial benchmarks focus almost exclusively on regulatory filings, neglecting market dynamics and cross-signal interactions. We introduce FinTradeBench, a benchmark of 1,400 questions covering NASDAQ-100 stocks over a ten-year window. To holistically evaluate financial reasoning, FinTradeBench includes questions in three reasoning categories: fundamentals-focused, trading-signal-focused, and hybrid questions that require cross-signal reasoning. Our benchmark starts with 150 expert-authored seed questions, which are then scaled to 1400. To ensure reliable evaluation, we create a Core400 subset in which every item is expert-reviewed. In zero-shot (No-RAG) response generation, we observe that all models perform substantially worse on trading-focused questions compared to fundamental-focused questions. To identify failure causes, we evaluate LLMs under controlled settings including retrieval-based baselines, tool-assisted computation, oracle conditions, and time-series-specialized pipelines. LLMs' capabilities to do computation and evidence selection contribute most to reasoning failures. Additionally, retrieval significantly improves trading-signal reasoning but does not improve fundamental reasoning, where identifying relevant signals remains the dominant failure mode. Finally, rather than relying on a small, fine-tuned model for time-series reasoning, it is better to summarize the time-series data and leverage LLMs' reasoning capabilities.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages main text, 36 pages total (including references and appendix). 9 figures, 31 tables. Preprint under review. Code and data will be made available upon publication</p></details> |
| **[LLM-as-Jev: LLMs Are Already Jev-Style Decision Models -- When and How to Fine-Tune Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02076v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Jev-style decision models have gained surging traction by making decision-making dramatically faster and more efficient, returning categorical probability distributions over predefined options without generating free-form text. However, because Jev's architecture and training methodologies remain proprietary, how to replicate these capabilities effectively remains an open question. In this work, we investigate the extent to which general-purpose LLMs already possess this capability out of the box, and when fine-tuning is actually necessary. We present LLM-as-Jev, an architecture-preserving framework offering both a training-free inference recipe and a fine-tuning objective with KL-divergence anchors. Across Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3-0.6B, we find that modern LLMs are inherently effective decision models: off the shelf, frozen models match or exceed existing training-free readouts on the same 4B backbone, scale to arbitrary option counts, and natively handle multimodal decisions. They also seamlessly inherit the backbone's thinking mode: while Jev was introduced as a System 1 model, "Thinking Jev" leverages budgeted test-time reasoning to boost accuracy on challenging tasks without training. Fine-tuning provides targeted gains for smaller models and complex routing, while our KL anchors prevent behavioral degradation in general text generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[OracleProto: A Reproducible Framework for Benchmarking LLM Forecasting via Knowledge Cutoffs and Temporal Masking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03762v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forecasting is a composite capability encompassing information gathering, evidence integration, situational assessment, and decision-making. Large language models (LLMs) combine reasoning and tool use, offering new avenues for automated forecasting and decision support. Yet LLM forecasting evaluation faces a structural challenge: live benchmarks support prospective assessment, but limited question reuse and changing information conditions make results difficult to reproduce and compare; retrospective benchmarks offer reproducibility but risk information leakage. We introduce OracleProto, a reproducible framework for evaluating LLM forecasting in a controlled, temporally masked environment. Through model knowledge cutoff constraints and temporal masking, OracleProto constructs a temporal sandbox for real-world web retrieval, audits information boundaries through leakage detection, and reconstructs resolved events as forecasting instances. Discrete answer normalization and hierarchical scoring enable unified evaluation. We evaluate 27 model-configuration pairs on 300 FutureX-Past-derived questions over 24,300 prediction trials, assessing forecasting quality, sampling stability, and cost efficiency under controlled information boundaries. An audit finds only 1.37% residual leakage in retrieval results after temporal masking and leakage filtering. OracleProto retains resolved forecasting tasks as reusable evaluation resources under explicit temporal boundaries, supporting continual accumulation of forecasting datasets and systematic evaluation of forecasting capabilities. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OracleProto-2E3A/.</p></details> |  |
| **[What Users See Is Not What Models Read: Split-View PDFs in Document-to-LLM Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15020v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Document-to-LLM applications typically read uploaded PDFs by first translating them into text through an extraction layer whose output is not ordinarily exposed to users. We show that this layer enables split-view PDFs: one document can have two semantic views before model reasoning. By mining specification-permitted or implementation-tolerated representation gaps at the PDF render/extract boundary, we instantiate 25 extraction gaps (EG) in which extractors return attacker-controlled or extractor-dependent text while the rendered page shows benign or different content. The gaps form four families: semantic overrides, hidden semantic injection, reading-order splits, and font-decoding splits, and 14 gaps have no exact path/mechanism-level match in prior PDF-to-LLM attacks. We evaluate these gaps on 16 PDF processing stacks and 7 commercial LLM services. Each gap causes render-extract divergence on at least one stack. Under a gap-level exposure criterion, every evaluated service exposes at least one gap, with 12/25 to 21/25 exposed gaps. Exposure is driven mainly by the ingestion stack -- not model identity alone. We further show that tested safety filters cover only selected hidden-text constructions. To support triage, we develop a static screening scanner whose rules trigger on all 25 benchmark gaps, and discuss dual-view consistency as a longer-term defense direction.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (S&P 2027)</p></details> |
| **[Can an Agent Learn to Predict Its Own Failure? Outcome-Grounded Confidence Calibration in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18614v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Controlling an acting LLM agent means deciding, at each step of a long-horizon task, whether to trust its next action, abstain, or hand off to a human. That decision needs a per-step confidence signal predicting whether the current rollout will eventually succeed. The agent's own self-reported verbalized confidence is the natural candidate, but existing reinforcement learning methods calibrate it against the correctness of a decision at the current step. We propose a method to calibrate it against the eventual outcome of a multi-step episode: under GRPO, the group of rollouts for a task supplies success labels, and a proper scoring rule scores each step's confidence against the realized outcome. In offline RL on recorded trajectories of a Qwen2.5-7B agent in ALFWorld, TextWorld, and WebShop environments, the AUROC of step confidence against episode outcome rises from near chance to 0.68-0.85, and the top-10% of steps the agent is most confident about succeed ~4.3x more often than average. In online RL on the live ALFWorld agent, four calibration rewards improve early-step AUROC, expected calibration error goes from 0.42 to below 0.12, and selective execution improves over both the untrained agent and a format-only RL control, without degrading task success. We also introduce an evaluation protocol that separates episode depth, task difficulty, and attempt-level foresight, which shows where the gain lies: the trained agent recognizes from its first step which tasks it will fail, while telling a succeeding attempt from a failing one at the same task is harder, with some early-window signal emerging on TextWorld.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Projector Training in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18598v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal LLMs are typically trained by first aligning a vision encoder with a language model via projector training on large image-caption datasets, followed by joint training of the full multimodal model. Motivated by these findings, we propose PORTAL, a training-free projector initialization that requires neither paired image-caption data nor gradient-based optimization, but only per-modality summary statistics. PORTAL computes an optimal-transport-based map between Gaussian approximations of the vision and language feature distributions on a shared principal subspace, so that the projector training stage can be skipped entirely. Without any paired data, PORTAL matches the standard pipeline trained on 558k image-caption pairs across six LLM backbones, two vision encoders, and 16 benchmarks. It outperforms that pipeline in $3$ of $7$ (vision encoder, LLM) settings and reaches an average $98\%$ of its score across the other four.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 camera-ready</p></details> |
| **[Compare to Correct: Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment for LLM Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18583v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sampling-based test-time scaling enhances LLM reasoning by aggregating multiple reasoning paths for the same query. Existing methods use answer frequencies or path confidence to compute candidate scores, which do not explicitly characterize comparisons between competing answers. In this way, our proposed Pairwise-Guided Logit Adjustment (PGLA) framework allows comparative evidence to compensate for weaker voting support and correct voting-based ranking errors. Experiments on 10 challenging benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, knowledge reasoning, and code generation with 7 models ranging from 1.7B to 32B parameters speak to the effectiveness of this plug-and-play term. Additional evaluations with external reward scores and online sampling further demonstrate its applicability.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM Agents for Automated Post-Training via Structured Recipe Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18572v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated post-training transforms pre-trained large language models (LLMs) into models capable of handling diverse domains by automatically constructing and optimizing a recipe of coupled decisions over data sources, data processing, cross-domain composition, and training configuration under a limited experimental budget. Existing automation either searches predefined recipe spaces that enable systematic optimization but remain fixed rather than adapting their available choices to the target tasks, or uses open-ended agents that discover task-specific choices but leave them implicit in trial-level edits, limiting their systematic reuse and recombination across trials. To fill this gap, we introduce optimization over a task-adapted recipe space, a novel paradigm that can adapt the recipe space itself to the target tasks while representing its choices explicitly for systematic reuse, recombination, and joint optimization. We instantiate this paradigm with Structured Recipe Optimization (SRO), comprising an LLM-based Recipe-Space Designer that constructs the task-adapted recipe space and a Multi-Domain Recipe Optimizer that uses structured trial evidence to guide LLM candidate construction and multi-objective Bayesian trial selection. | **[Revisiting Cauchy-Schwarz Norm Bounds for SDC Detection in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18557v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) execute billions of floating-point operations per output token across many hardware accelerators. Algorithm-Based Fault Tolerance (ABFT) is the state-of-the-art technique for protecting matrix multiplication from such faults, but it incurs non-negligible computational overhead and requires thresholds to distinguish hardware-induced errors from floating-point roundoff. In this paper, we revisit Cauchy--Schwarz Norm Bounds (CSNB) as a low-overhead alternative for detecting SDC during LLM inference. We compare CSNB against ABFT through controlled fault injection and evaluate the runtime overhead of both verification and threshold determination across multiple model sizes, batch sizes, and generation lengths. Our results show that, on average, CSNB retains 99% of the fault detection capability of ABFT for the evaluated dense linear layers while reducing verification overhead by 87% and threshold determination overhead by 67%. For Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) router gate computations, however, CSNB retains only 55% of the fault detection capability of ABFT. These findings suggest that CSNB is a promising low-overhead alternative for protecting dense linear layers in LLM inference, while checksum-based verification remains preferable for the evaluated MoE router gate.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Spectra to Joint Schedules in LLM Pre-training: 3+3(+2) Scaling-Law Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40148v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Power-law learning curves are often treated as fixed properties of a model and its data, although learning-rate and batch-size schedules can change the observed loss. We study this dependence in noisy online SGD with linear random features. Conditional on the representation, an exact Volterra equation separates two response components: a forcing term that propagates unresolved target error and a memory kernel that propagates stochastic-error injections. We prove that either component follows a power law if and only if its cumulative weighted spectral mass has the corresponding low-spectrum scaling; individual eigenvalues and target coefficients need not obey coordinatewise power laws. Under a joint schedule, intrinsic time $T_t=\sum_{s<t}η_s$ controls optimization progress, while $r_t=B_t/η_t$ controls noise injection. Their interaction yields sharp conditions under which a schedule preserves, changes, or destroys the clean power law, together with a memory ceiling on noise reduction. The power-law random-feature model realizes this mechanism in $3+3(+2)$ propagation regimes with phase-dependent compute rates. Controlled nanoGPT experiments show that (1) learning-rate and batch-size schedules with matched $B/η$ paths are nearly equivalent in intrinsic time, (2) a forcing-memory surrogate accurately predicts loss across schedules, and (3) its fitted exponents across real-world datasets identify the regime of LLMs in $3+3(+2)$ map. | **[Lost in Implicit Routing: How LLM Agents Fail to Load Relevant Skills at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18522v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reusable skills let LLM agents load task-specific instructions on demand, but they make execution depend on implicit routing over skill metadata. These findings point to semantic interference from descriptions and instruction interference when skill choice competes with task execution. We also evaluate explicit preselection as a practical mitigation, including its coverage and latency trade-offs. | **[A Global View of How LLMs Value Human Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18515v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as personal assistants; however, little is known about how they capture national-level preferences on a global scale. | **[ECMR: Evidence-Aware Deliberation Control for Multi-Agent LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17869v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent Large Language Model (LLM) systems typically commit to a deliberation protocol before inference. We introduce ECMR, an evidence-aware controller over frozen role-conditioned LLMs. At each step, ECMR summarizes answer concentration, support diversity and independence, contradictions, role behavior, verification history, and remaining budget, then selects whether to propose, challenge, verify, arbitrate, diversify, correct a role, or stop. We formulate this decision as finite-horizon budgeted control and derive sufficient conditions under which state conditioning, risk-triggered verification, and role correction improve cost-adjusted utility. Across a declared held-out evaluation of 9,000 records from MedQA, CaseHOLD, and FinQA, ECMR-Full increases pooled accuracy from 62.1% to 84.9%, reduces false-consensus rate from 40.7% to 17.0%, and raises evidence-support rate from 58.0% to 88.7%. This paper examines systems optimizations for LLM-based compression, including multistream pipelined arithmetic coding and target-interval capture, and evaluates their trade-offs across inference engines, configurations and datasets. Notably, we obtain a speedup of 18.84x compared to prior work's Transformers implementation and a maximum recorded inference speed of 181 kilobytes per second at a compression ratio of 7.17, when running on an NVIDIA A100.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 2 figures. Published at ADMS 2026, co-located with VLDB 2026, Boston, USA</p></details> |
| **[Decision Models vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges: Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29769v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges now score outputs against rubrics, in benchmarks and as training rewards. Decision models return probabilities over permitted answers with a confidence score but no free text. Two such models are Jev and an open reproduction, Perplexity's Decider. We compare each with three flash-tier LLM judges on nine panels from seven benchmarks with human judgments, giving every judge identical criterion texts. The LLM judges run in two setups, reading a whole rubric at once or one criterion at a time. Our findings suggest both decision models can often stand in for them. The LLM judges cost 16 to 357 times as much and take 5 to 350 times as long, yet in both setups each decision model trails them significantly in at most 6 of 27 comparisons. The two kinds of judge err alike, agreeing more with one another than with the human-annotated judgment labels on ordinal criteria. Over 85% of flash-tier LLM verdicts on either decision model's most confident errors repeat them, where independent errors would give about half. Each decision model's confidence ranks its errors on most ordinal panels, so each is a natural first stage for a cascade deferring uncertain verdicts to an LLM judge. Yet with flash-tier fallbacks, such cascades gained a mere 2.7 points at best over the strongest single judge, even with oracle thresholds, suggesting they mainly save cost. A cascade needs a fallback that is right where its first stage errs, but the LLM judges are mostly wrong in the same places as Jev, and the Decider is already the strongest single judge on most panels. Even each vendor's higher-tier model repeats those errors, and as a fallback it lifted no cascade more than 1.7 points above both tiers' strongest single judge. Holding across setups, effort levels, and tiers, these findings suggest designing future decision models afresh to get right what LLM judges get wrong, and vice versa.</p></details> | <details><summary>58 pa...</summary><p>58 pages, 11 figures, 37 tables</p></details> |
| **[Dissociating Answerability from Extraction in LLM Question Answering over Radiology Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17576v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Answering questions from radiology reports involves two decisions: whether the report contains an answer and which span gives it. A false answer presents an unsupported claim as fact; an over-abstention hides what the report says. Yet RadQA results are reported as a single F1 that merges both. We propose an evaluation method that separates them, reporting false-answer and over-abstention rates beside F1 and splitting F1 changes into extraction and answerability terms. We apply it to 13 LLMs on the RadQA test set, zero-shot and with three cumulative demonstration sets, the last adding an unanswerable example. Models with similar F1 differed more than twofold in false-answer rate. The unanswerable example changed when models answered, not how well they extracted: 12 of 13 models abstained more often, and three-quarters of the answers they withdrew had F1 $\geq$ 0.5. Within models, false answers showed no clear association with extraction quality, but acting on the decision itself helped: keeping an answer only when enough other models also answered lowered the zero-shot false-answer rate by 4 points without a detectable F1 change. Because the two decisions can change independently, we suggest radiology QA evaluations report them separately, not through F1 alone.</p></details> |  |
| **[Parason: Revealing Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24658v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling test-time reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but standard autoregressive decoding still executes long reasoning traces sequentially, creating severe latency for difficult tasks (up to days and weeks). Parallel reasoning offers a natural remedy. However, prior systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism, where the model learns to decompose a high-level task into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. This approach overlooks another pervasive form of parallelism: Trial Parallelism, where multiple speculative attempts explore, verify, and aggregate competing hypotheses in parallel. In this paper, we introduce Parason, which reveals and learns both forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning. Our analysis identifies Trial Parallelism as the majority of parallelizable reasoning computation (65.5% in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE), and it becomes increasingly dominant on hard problems. Guided by this taxonomy, Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories with a context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios. At inference time, Parason executes the learned parallel structure through tool calls, translating theoretical savings to real-world wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25 show that Parason achieves an average acceleration about 1.7$\times$ while maintaining competitive accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Many Steps into One: Reasoning Contraction and the Limits of Safe Memory Reuse for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17544v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thought templates, skill libraries, agent memories and caches turn many reasoning steps into one. We call this reasoning contraction. A contracted memory saves reasoning steps and errors until its facts change, then quietly answers wrong. Most systems trust memories by counting successes and failures. We prove that such counters are badly tuned change detectors: a symmetric counter notices a partial change only after a power of its false-alarm horizon, and an unclipped one may never notice it. On five LLMs answering multi-hop questions over edited facts, ExpeL- and ACE-style counters serve 7.0-13.5x more stale answers than certified memories given the same information, and on code tasks with an exact verifier 35-73x more. Safety also has a price. When facts change and each memory is judged only by its own verified outcomes, one number, the certification load, sets how much reasoning any safe memory can skip, and under Zipf demand it yields a scaling law. Our recipe weighs each memory's failures by its own likelihood ratio, certifies its utility against live reasoning, and restarts the certificate when facts change, with the likelihood-ratio detector as the safety net for unannounced changes; for large edits it also invalidates the memory.</p></details> | 57 pages |

However, most evaluation frameworks focus only on whether a user request is unsafe. This motivates us to go beyond detection to localize the evidence that pushes the conversation toward unsafe trajectories. We introduce MTAG-Bench, a multi-turn guardrail attribution dataset with behavioral validation and counterfactual supervision containing 4,167 conversations. We also introduce MTAG-Probe, a hierarchical attribution model for turn- and span-level evidence localization with conversation-level safety detection. MTAG-Probe improves turn-level F1 by 28.1 and 24.1 points (79.0% and 61.0% relative) over self-attribution by the model that produced the unsafe response and the LLM safety-judge attribution, respectively. Removing MTAG-Probe's top-ranked evidence within budgets of 15% and 20% of user words per conversation changes 50.6% and 60.8% of Llama Guard decisions on Geometry from unsafe to safe, respectively. On the Challenge subset, frontier models including Gemini-3-Pro, GPT-5, and Claude-Opus-4.5 reach 30.4%, 26.4%, and 10.4% respectively, leaving substantial headroom, while leading open-weight models such as Qwen3-235B, GPT-OSS-120B, and Kimi-2.5 remain below 15%. Notably, beyond standard problem solving, Soohak introduces a refusal subset that probes a capability intrinsic to research mathematics: recognizing ill-posed problems and pausing rather than producing confident but unjustified answers. On this subset, no model exceeds 50%, identifying refusal as a new optimization target that current models do not directly address. To prevent contamination, the dataset will be publicly released in late 2026, with model evaluations available upon request in the interim.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under review, For questions or model-evaluation requests, contact $guijin.son@snu.ac.kr$</p></details> |
| **[Towards Green AI: Evaluating Energy Efficiency for Quantized LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17531v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While it is known that quantization of large language models (LLMs) reduces memory usage via lower-bit weights, studies quantifying the resulting impact on energy usage and carbon intensity are scarce. Furthermore, we show that quantization strategies become marginally more energy-efficient relative to full-precision models as batch sizes increase, though gains are modest. Notably, fused-kernel implementations such as EETQ int8 and Bitsandbytes int8 offer the highest energy savings, up to 4x compared to FP32 on short text generation, with negligible accuracy loss. Finally, we observe that energy usage closely tracks runtime on our evaluated benchmarks, indicating that, in practice, latency optimization can serve as a reliable proxy for reducing the environmental footprint of LLM services. We conclude with suggested directions for strategically selecting low-carbon quantization strategies based on specific inference requirements.</p></details> |  |
| **[Behavioral History Outperforms Descriptions of the Person for LLM Synthetic Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03998v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as synthetic personas representing survey respondents. | **[RogueMerge: Robust and Unified Attacks against LLM Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03344v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model merging composes specialized capabilities into a single LLM by aggregating task-specific LLMs sourced from unverified public platforms, exposing a critical supply-chain attack surface: Because any malicious behavior can be encoded into a task vector, and merging grants third-party vectors direct write access to model weights, an attacker-provided task vector can enable or amplify diverse downstream threats. (ii) Attackers have no knowledge of the victim's merging configurations, causing a static attack vector optimized in isolation to be easily diluted or neutralized. (iii) Practical threat induction must generalize to attack prompts unseen during optimization, which static vectors cannot adequately encode. In light of these gaps, we present RogueMerge, the first unified attack framework addressing all three challenges. To handle autoregressive generation, we replace static arithmetic with a joint optimization that explicitly enforces attack success after merging. To better simulate unknown merging settings, we formulate attack injection as a stochastic min-max optimization problem and solve it via meta-gradient descent. To generalize across heterogeneous attack prompts, we employ distributionally robust optimization and derive a tractable first-order Taylor approximation at LLM scale, with a provable error bound. Across four threats, six merging algorithms, and over 170 merged LLMs, RogueMerge consistently outperforms prior attacks. It also remains stable across diverse merging settings and resists standard defenses.</p></details> | <details><summary>To ap...</summary><p>To appear in IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (S&P), 2027</p></details> |

In a held-out-words regression, behavioral similarity (especially forced choice) predicts unseen hidden-state similarities beyond lexical baselines and cross-model consensus, indicating that behavior-only measurements retain recoverable information about internal semantic geometry. Finally, we discuss implications for the ability of behavioral tasks to uncover hidden cognitive states.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at the 43rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2026), Seoul, South Korea. 27 pages, 16 figures, 6 tables. Code: https://github.com/schiekiera/llm-association-geometry. Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/schiekiera/llm-association-geometry</p></details> |
| **[SchemaWeaver: Accelerating On-Device Mobile Agentic AI via Schema-Guided Parallel LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17436v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) on mobile devices can act as agents that call external software tools to complete complex tasks, but the major challenge of supporting on-device mobile agentic AI with LLMs is the long latency of LLM tool calling on mobile devices. To address this limitation, in this paper we present SchemaWeaver, a new on-device mobile agentic AI technique that enforces schema-guided parallel decoding in the decode phase of LLM tool calling, to reduce its latency by producing multiple tokens in each decode pass. For model selection, we design an efficient ZK proof for semantic search. For rewriting, local checks enforce message preservation and additions from committed banks. On real-world workloads, VeriRouter's model-selection and rewrite proofs average 21.92~s and 4.24~s, yielding 1,307$\times$ and 101$\times$ speedups over the fastest completing baselines. | **[Reading the Room: Foundations, Design, and Challenges of Normative Competence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10906v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human communities are governed by normative systems: shared standards that produce \textit{norms} dictating acceptable behavior, enforced through community sanctioning. Aligning increasingly autonomous AI systems with these norms is a central alignment challenge, complicated by the fact that norms are vast in number, change quickly, and are often arbitrary (e.g., dress or language conventions). Thus, alignment requires \textit{normative competence}: the ability to discern from interaction alone what norms a community enforces without relying on static pretrained knowledge. We introduce a multi-agent community debate setting, where access to debate is governed by synthetic norms, to study normative competence in isolation from pretraining exposure. We show that baseline LLM agents fail to learn norms even when doing so would improve their accuracy. We then experiment with various \textit{normative modules} -- architectural components for norm inference -- finding that norm-following is highly sensitive to both the style of norm and the model powering the normative module, suggesting a lack of generalizability. Furthermore, when idiosyncratic, non-normative behaviors accompany the true norm, LLM agents exhibit an unselective attribution failure: they indiscriminately copy idiosyncratic noise alongside enforced rules, a pattern that persists even when imitating unnecessary behaviors is explicitly penalized. To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to operationalize and evaluate normative competence in LLMs, demonstrating that current AI systems excel at behavioral mimicry but lack the capacity to discern socially enforced order.</p></details> |  |
| **[EPOCH: Coverage-Aware Evolution with Experiential Preference Optimization for LLM-based Automated Heuristic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17379v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based automated heuristic design (AHD) discovers heuristics for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs), where an LLM generates candidates in an evolutionary search. We propose EPOCH, which makes instance coverage the objective of the evolutionary search and fine-tunes the generator to internalize reflection. Parent selection, operator allocation, and stagnation recovery all optimize how well the heuristics jointly cover the training instances, so the search returns a complementary heuristic ensemble. The generator learns from its own search by experiential preference optimization. A teacher LLM returns a natural-language insight on the generated heuristics, and the generator produces new heuristics with this insight. Since DPO only teaches the generator to rank these improvements, a reflective self-distillation (RSD) term makes it more likely to generate them on its own, and the gains accumulate over the long horizon of the search. However, these defenses are expensive and offer limited protection against attacks that are robust to semantic censorship. First, we introduce representational commitment, which measures hidden-state similarity across repeated runs at the same step. This provides an internal measure for studying when agent behavior stabilizes. Second, we establish the measure's scope. Shared evidence explains much of the observed association, the HotpotQA result disappears on hard questions, and correct and confidently wrong questions overlap. Camera-ready version, Workshop on Agent Behavior at COLM 2026</p></details> |
| **[TRAP: Tree-Guided Reinforcement-Augmented Probing for Automated LLM Jailbreaking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17313v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aligned large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak prompts that circumvent their safety guardrails, making the identification of these vulnerabilities important for understanding and mitigating model weaknesses. Existing automated jailbreak methods explore prompt candidates or reuse attack experience, but generally do not learn over a persistent hierarchy that connects related jailbreak strategies, such as role-play, encoding, and obfuscation. Retrieval-augmented prompt construction translates the selected strategy into an attack for the current objective using relevant successful examples. Code: https://github.com/Rezamirbagheri110/TRAP</p></details> |

## graph neural network
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Emergent Loop Geometry in Gauge-Equivariant Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18742v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gauge-equivariant graph neural networks generate ordered products of gauge links through local message passing, but the geometry of these internal structures is not captured by the receptive field alone. We investigate the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks generated by recursive node- and edge-feature operations, independently of network training. Analytical and numerical results reveal a separation between rapid growth of contour length and much slower spatial expansion: deeper compositions generate increasingly long paths through repeated traversal of relatively compact regions. Loop expansions of fermionic observables provide a physical interpretation of this hierarchy, showing how spatial locality remains compatible with high-order dependence on nearby gauge fields. These findings identify complementary roles of network depth in extending spatial reach and enriching the representations constructed within that reach, providing a geometrical perspective on learning lattice gauge observables.</p></details> | 16 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Tensor-reduced atomic density representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01705v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Density based representations of atomic environments that are invariant under Euclidean symmetries have become a widely used tool in the machine learning of interatomic potentials, broader data-driven atomistic modelling and the visualisation and analysis of materials datasets.The standard mechanism used to incorporate chemical element information is to create separate densities for each element and form tensor products between them. This leads to a steep scaling in the size of the representation as the number of elements increases. Graph neural networks, which do not explicitly use density representations, escape this scaling by mapping the chemical element information into a fixed dimensional space in a learnable way. We recast this approach as tensor factorisation by exploiting the tensor structure of standard neighbour density based descriptors. In doing so, we form compact tensor-reduced representations whose size does not depend on the number of chemical elements, but remain systematically convergeable and are therefore applicable to a wide range of data analysis and regression tasks.</p></details> | 6 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Contrastive Metric Learning for Point Cloud Segmentation in Highly Granular Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23356v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a novel clustering approach for point-cloud segmentation based on supervised contrastive metric learning (CML). Rather than predicting cluster assignments or object-centric variables, the method learns a latent representation in which points belonging to the same object are embedded nearby while unrelated points are separated. Clusters are then reconstructed using a density-based readout in the learned metric space, decoupling representation learning from cluster formation and enabling flexible inference. The approach is evaluated on simulated data from a highly granular calorimeter, where the task is to separate highly overlapping particle showers represented as sets of calorimeter hits. A direct comparison with object condensation (OC) is performed using identical graph neural network backbones and equal latent dimensionality, isolating the effect of the learning objective. The CML method produces a more stable and separable embedding geometry for both electromagnetic and hadronic particle showers, leading to improved local neighbourhood consistency, a more reliable separation of overlapping showers, and better generalization when extrapolating to unseen multiplicities and energies. This translates directly into higher reconstruction efficiency and purity, particularly in high-multiplicity regimes, as well as improved energy resolution. In mixed-particle environments, CML maintains strong performance, suggesting robust learning of the shower topology, while OC exhibits significant degradation. These results demonstrate that similarity-based representation learning combined with density-based aggregation is a promising alternative to object-centric approaches for point cloud segmentation in highly granular detectors.</p></details> | 12 pages, 5 figures |

On the other hand, we show theoretically that dense embeddings precomputed for graph nodes, even with augmented structure and neighborhood-aware features, can require embedding dimensions comparable to the size of the graph to answer families of knowledge graph queries. This limitation can be overcome with query-adaptive embeddings under certain conditions, and there are graph neural network (GNN) variants that can do so. However, processing the whole graph with a GNN can also incur substantial memory and computational costs, and it requires dense ground-truth labels indicating whether each node answers the query. Straightforward $k$-hop selection around anchor nodes can also be problematic: small $k$ limits the nodes we can see, and even with small $k$ values such as three and four, the $k$-hop subgraph can grow substantially. In this work, we devise a new approach using Graph Transformers and reinforcement learning that is much less demanding in memory and computation, can run on a CPU at inference time as a first-stage retriever, and requires feedback on only a small number of nodes at a time during training. We show that this method is competitive with methods that fine-tune LLMs to retrieve information from KGs. We call our method SeedER (Seed-Expand-Retrieve), and position it primarily as a first-stage retriever that can run with modest computational resources.</p></details> |  |
| **[Aligning Multimodal Patient Evidence with Biomedical Knowledge Graphs for Clinical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06685v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical questions often depend on linking a patient's multimodal evidence to external biomedical knowledge, yet existing predictive systems rarely represent such links explicitly, so they can neither be traced to their evidence sources nor removed to measure their contributions. On held-out five-candidate ranking, MM-KG outperforms MindMap by +0.131 Hits@1 and leads an adapted GraphCare on the items that require consulting the patient, and deleting the single answer-bearing relation from the retrieved packet returns Hits@1 to the no-knowledge baseline. | **[Inverse Cross-spectral Neural Networks for Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06630v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>CoVariance Neural Networks and their extensions have emerged as effective tools for processing multivariate data, deriving graph shift operators directly from second-order statistics. In this work, we introduce Inverse Cross-Spectral Neural Networks (iCSNNs), a class of graph neural networks for stationary multivariate time series whose shift operators are the inverse cross-spectral density (iCSD) matrices. Leveraging spectral smoothness, frequencies are grouped into bands sharing a single iCSD operator, yielding a compact parametrisation that retains the frequency-dependent structure of the process. We further propose a joint learning procedure to estimate both the Fourier-domain dependence structure and the iCSNN parameters, adapting the iCSD operators to the downstream task. | **[TripleBound: Triplet-Guided Heterogeneous Graph Learning for Microservice Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11212v2)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud computing and DevOps have made microservices a common architecture for scalable, maintainable software systems. Per-metric analysis reveals trade-offs: gains in structural modularity and inter-partition coupling are accompanied by higher entity distribution imbalance on some datasets. Alternative composite weightings preserve TripleBound's first-place ranking on AcmeAir and DayTrader but not on JPetStore, showing that the aggregate ranking is metric-dependent.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 4 figures. Replication package available on GitHub</p></details> |
| **[GPlaceRL: An Open-Source Graph Reinforcement Learning Framework for Detailed Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06489v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising approach for placement optimization, particularly when combined with graph neural networks (GNNs) that capture circuit connectivity. In this paper, we present GPlaceRL, an open-source graph reinforcement learning framework for detailed placement refinement. Across five placement benchmarks, the best greedy evaluation results achieve HPWL improvements ranging from $3.27\%$ to $32.87\%$. | **[FairProp: Fair Node Representation Learning via Differentiable Propagation Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06484v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph neural networks (GNNs) are the standard tool for node representation learning and are increasingly used in high-stakes settings. Our node classification bound is provably no looser than the closest prior result. Across all three tasks, the analysis identifies two distinct sources of bias: the separation between group means and the within-group covariance of the final representations. Building on this insight, we embed fairness into propagation itself by augmenting the convex smoothing problem underlying APPNP with a convex group-mean constraint and a within-group covariance regularizer. Unfolding projected gradient descent on this problem yields FairProp, whose layers pair a propagation step with a closed-form projection and which provably converges linearly to the unique fair optimum. Experiments on three tasks show that FairProp, even with exact group-mean equalization alone, provides a strong inductive bias that achieves excellent fairness-utility trade-offs against strong baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Stability-Shaped Deep Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06344v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In deep graph neural networks, increasing depth enlarges the receptive field but often leads to over-smoothing, where node representations tend to align. Guided by this theory, we further propose Stability-Shaped Deep Graph Learning (SDGL) to mitigate over-smoothing in deep GNNs. SDGL has two complementary instantiations: one induces controlled Turing instability to replace synchronization with spatial pattern formation, and the other maintains stable near-critical propagation. Experiments on diverse node- and graph-level benchmarks demonstrate the improved depth scaling and consistent accuracy gains over strong baselines, including graphs exhibiting long-range dependencies.</p></details> |  |

| **[Graph Neural Network-Driven Deep Reinforcement Learning for Scalable RIS Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06295v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reconfigurable Intelligent Surfaces (RISs) offer a promising paradigm to mitigate blockage and extend millimeter-wave coverage in 6G multi-cell networks. | **[Answer Set Networks: Casting Answer Set Programming into Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14814v2)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although Answer Set Programming (ASP) allows constraining neural-symbolic (NeSy) systems, its employment is hindered by the prohibitive costs of computing stable models and the CPU-bound nature of state-of-the-art solvers. | **[HardCore Generation: Generating Hard UNSAT Problems for Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.18778v2)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficiently determining the satisfiability of a boolean equation -- known as the SAT problem for brevity -- is crucial in various industrial problems. Recently, the advent of deep learning methods has introduced significant potential for enhancing SAT solving. However, a major barrier to the advancement of this field has been the scarcity of large, realistic datasets. The majority of current public datasets are either randomly generated or extremely limited, containing only a few examples from unrelated problem families. These datasets are inadequate for meaningful training of deep learning methods. In light of this, researchers have started exploring generative techniques to create data that more accurately reflect SAT problems encountered in practical situations. These methods have so far suffered from either the inability to produce challenging SAT problems or time-scalability obstacles. In this paper we address both by identifying and manipulating the key contributors to a problem's ``hardness'', known as cores. Although some previous work has addressed cores, the time costs are unacceptably high due to the expense of traditional heuristic core detection techniques. We introduce a fast core detection procedure that uses a graph neural network. Our empirical results demonstrate that we can efficiently generate problems that remain hard to solve and retain key attributes of the original example problems. We show via experiment that the generated synthetic SAT problems can be used in a data augmentation setting to provide improved prediction of solver runtimes.</p></details> |  |
| **[Graph-Based Recognition of Simulated Train-Driver States From Facial and Upper-Body Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07083v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Driver fatigue poses a significant challenge to railway safety, with traditional systems like the dead-man switch offering limited and basic alertness checks.
